1. **Identify the black nodes:**
   - The black nodes are R and P.

2. **Find immediate neighboring nodes:**
   - The immediate neighboring nodes of R are C, J, and O.
   - The immediate neighboring nodes of P are I and Q.

3. **Trace paths between the two black nodes:**
   - From R to C, C connects to B and G.
   - From C to B, B connects to E, S, A, F, and T.
   - From E to A, A connects back to S and F.
   - From A to F, F connects back to T and S.
   - From B to F, F connects back to T and S.
   - From B to T, T connects back to F and S.
   - From R to J, J connects to G, H, and I.
   - H connects to I.
   - I connects to Q.
   - Q connects to P.

4. **Evaluate paths:**
   - Any path from R to P must go through either G or I.
   - From J, which connects to R, J connects to I.
   - If we remove the edges (I, P) and (I, Q), then the connection is broken.
   - Otherwise, a path might go through O to B, then to F, and to P through Q.
   - Even if we consider complex paths, removing both edges from nodes connecting R and J, and P from I suffices to break all paths.

5. **Determine the minimum number of edges to remove:**
   - The bottleneck is mostly via the nodes G, J, and I.
   - Removing the edges (J, G) and (J, I) from R towards I and P's connections is minimal.
   - This ensures no remaining path exists between R and P.

Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect the two black nodes is:

Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(R, J), (Q, I)].

    - B connects to both O and S, forming a path between them.
    - C forms a connection path with O to S through B.
    - N also connects back to B, where it forms another full path to S
    - To find a bottleneck we note that O connects via B, but also via C and N. Therefore, we could either disconnect all edges from N or C and the connecting path between B and N would vanish.

4. **Analyze edges to remove**:
    - Alternatively, we can focus on removing edges directly connected to S, specifically (E, S), (F, S), (B, S).
    - If we disconnect (B, S) both the paths through O via (C,B) and (N,B) vanish. We also consider the additional bottleneck via (C, B), and (N, B). When removing (B, S) and (C, B), paths via N to B or C are gone. Removing (B, S) is thus not minimal.
    - Removing all the connecting edges to B suffices, as B acts as a bottleneck.
    - Removing the edges ((B, O), (B, C), (B, E)) or (O, N), (B,N), (F,B), etc. guarantees that paths between S and O vanishes.

5. **Determine the minimum set of edges to remove**:
    - To minimize the set, we remove the edge from either direction of the bottleneck at B.

Thus, the minimal set of edges we need to disconnect O and S is:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, O), (B, C), (B, E)].

1. **Identify the two black nodes:** R and I.
2. **Identify the immediate neighboring nodes of R and I:**
   - R is connected to F, E, and S.
   - I is connected to P, Q, H, and J.
3. **Identify the paths between R and I:**
   - R -> F -> B -> C -> T -> H -> I
   - R -> F -> B -> S -> A -> P -> I
   - R -> F -> B -> S -> A -> Q -> I
   - R -> E -> S -> A -> P -> I
   - R -> E -> S -> A -> Q -> I
   - R -> S -> A -> P -> I
   - R -> S -> A -> Q -> I

4. **Evaluate the found paths:**
   - All paths between R and I have to cross either F or E to reach B and then S.
   - Alternatively, all paths must cross S to reach A and then P or Q to reach I.

5. **Determine the minimum number of edges to remove:**
   - Removing the edge (R, S) will disconnect one main path.
   - Removing the edge (R, F) will disconnect the other path, but leaving another path via R-E-S.
   - Removing the edge (S, A) will also effectively cut the connection both ways.
   - Thus, removing edges (R, S) and (S, A) will effectively disconnect all paths between R and I.
